No Lovelier SpringCHAPTER I
T
Phe waiter hovered obsequiously, waiting to pull
back her chair. Sally picked up her purse and
the slim book of poetry from which she had read dur-
ing luncheon. She resisted an impulse to return the
waiter's wide beaming smile. A manner of cool de-
tachment, she thought, was more in keeping with
her new personality than the shy friendliness which
was her instinctive response to the impersonal friend-
liness in the Negro's ingratiating smile. She was
glad she had left a magnificent tip, though. The
waiter reminded her of Grandmother Shelby's Ben.
Besides, it was sure to impress the young man in
Harris tweeds at the table across the aisle.
She left the diner, walking with light sauntering
steps, a little marred by the motion of the train. She
was obliged to concentrate in order to maintain the
casual saunter. Her fingers were cold with excite-
ment and she had a hollow feeling inside. She was
still hungry-that was it. Luncheon had been only
aesthetically satisfying. She had waited for it long
after the usual hour, remaining in the Pullman until
9[10]
NO LOVELIER SPRING
the other girls from school, Paula Hartman, Susie
Lunt and Rosalie Archer, had returned from the
diner. They would hate her more than ever for
ignoring them. She didn't care. She wasn't going to
be associated, in the minds of the other people in the
car, with that fatuous trio, giggling and reading
movie magazines and stuffing themselves with
Martha Washington candy. Let them hate her. She
had Maida.
She wished Maida were here to give her moral
support. Sally stood in the vestibule of the Pullman
absently watching the Jersey landscape slide past.
Maida would have approved of the luncheon she'd
eaten; salad, melba toast and tea. That was enough
for Maida when she was being exotic. She conquered
a humiliating desire to return to the diner and order
a double portion of ice-cream and raisin pie.
The train rushed past a station platform. Trenton
already! Sally's heart gave a frightened lurch. She
couldn't do it! She wasn't ready yet to put her new
personality to the test of Mother's discerning ap-
praisal. It was real and her own only as long as she
kept it a secret. Her last year's suit was in her dress-
ing-case, the low-heeled pumps, the blouses with
ruffled collars. She would have time to change before
the train reached New York
But she had no hat except this, the red hat with
the dip over one eye which the saleslady had said
gave her costume the "color accent." It did, of
course. She had known in that thrilling momentNO LOVELIER SPRING
[11]
when the saleslady had tilted it over her forehead
and she'd seen her new permanent fluffing up behind,
that the red hat was right.
Perhaps, after all, it wasn't, though. Perhaps the
whole costume was affected and silly. Perhaps
Maida's enthusiasm had made her feel as she had
when she'd looked at herself in the flattering light
of the shop on Connecticut Avenue where Maida
bought her clothes. Maybe she really looked like her-
self, or like a show-off child pretending to be sophis-
ticated and smart. She had to know. However it
might affect her shaken self-consciousness, she had
to be sure.
The mirror in the dressing-room was narrow
and had a faintly greenish tint. Sally leaned toward
it, bracing herself with both hands pressed against
the shelf beneath. Confidence returned, bringing
with it a flooding sense of relief. It hadn't been
merely Maida's enthusiasm or the flattering lights
in the Washington shop. She looked-well, not beau-
tiful, but interesting, distinctive, a person in her
own right, she, Sarah Shelby Kent, not just Mother's
shadow. She looked, at last, like someone who might,
conceivably, have an exciting life of her own apart
from being the daughter of Laurel Kent.
She stood in blissful preoccupation, learning by
heart each detail of her altered appearance. Her
hair, black but with reddish glints, smoothed back
behind her ears, revealed the contours of her face
as the old way of doing it hadn't. Her eyes were[12] NO LOVELIER SPRING
enormous. They'd always been, of course, but she
hadn't considered that an asset. Mother's friends
had asked too often, in the playful way she detested,
"Where did you get those big black eyes, little girl?"
She had hated her eyes because they were a part of
her darkness and shyness, her difference from
Mother.
She liked them now. They looked bright and ex-
cited under the brim of the gay red hat. For almost
the first time in her life she didn't mind being dark.
Her skin was smooth and had a sheen like satin.
She was filling out, getting to look slender rather
than scrawny. The bones in her neck hardly showed
at all and her chest, under the sheer wool bodice
of her dress, didn't look quite so flat. Of course she'd
never be very tall. Maida said that wasn't important.
Juliet had been small and Lorna Doone and Babbie
and Mary Queen of Scots. Anyway, in her new high
heels, she'd be nearly as tall as Mother.
The dark wool dress was smart and becoming.
The frills at the throat and wrists managed, some-
how, to look more dashing than demure and the
shoulder cape had a jaunty swagger. Sally took a
lipstick from her bag and brightened her lips. The
girl in the beauty shop had told her she shouldn't use
rouge. Just sun-tan powder and lip salve, this bright
poppy red which almost exactly matched her hat.
The girl in the beauty shop had said she had Creole
coloring. She had worried about it until she con-
sulted the dictionary and found that Creoles wereNO LOVELIER SPRING [13]
natives of Louisiana descended from French or
Spanish ancestors. She'd had an idea that they were
connected with Negroes somehow.
Now that she knew what it meant, "Creole color-
ing" sounded glamorous and romantic, like her real
secret self which no one had known or could know
because she hadn't looked right. She'd known her
real self was there, hidden under the dark disguises
that Sally wore, like a fairy-tale princess, held by a
spell, waiting for the prince to release her. She'd
known, always, that this was how she was meant to
look, though she'd never told anyone-not even
Maida-until she was sure it was beginning to hap-
pen. It was happening now! Sally did a gloating
dance in the privacy of the dressing-room. She felt
so happy she wanted to shout. The beautiful right
thing for her was happening at last!
She returned to the Pullman, maintaining more
easily, now, the light casual saunter. She'd meant to
pass the girls from school without noticing them
but Susie Lunt hailed her.
"Going in for seclusion, Sally?" Malice glinted in
Susie's eyes, close-set under squinting brows. "Why
don't you wear black glasses? I recognized you al-
most at once. The name is Lunt, in case you don't re-
member."
Susie Lunt did look like a weasel-"a small
elongated, reddish-brown, carnivorous quadruped"
-Sally smiled, remembering the day she and Maida
had found, in the dictionary, that word picture of[14]
NO LOVELIER SPRING
Susie Lunt. Susie was misinterpreting the smile, of
course. She considered herself a wit.
The other girls were more friendly.
"Aren't you thrilled to be going home?" Paula
Hartman asked with bouncing enthusiasm and
Rosalie Archer murmured, "Divine dress, Sally."
"Thank you." Sally accepted the comment casually
but her breathing quickened a little. She hated her-
self for the pleasure which Rosalie's compliment
gave her. She supposed she would never outgrow
her admiration for blue eyes and yellow hair, though
Rosalie was only pretty in a pink and white "jeune
fille" sort of way. Rosalie hadn't a touch of the
"dark magic" which Maida said she, Sally, had.
Rosalie would never work much havoc in the world
though she was popular with boys still in prep school.
"Well-" Sally said and took a step away from the
three chairs turned to form an intimate grouping.
Paula detained her.
"Oh, Sally!" she said in a breathless rush, her
cheeks as pink as peonies, her straight hair wisping as
it always did from a bun at the nape of her neck.
"Mother is having a luncheon for me next Satur-
day. We're going to the matinee of your mother's
play. I suppose you've seen it a dozen times but I'd
love to have you. I mean if you'd like to come
"
"The play has closed," Sally said. "It closed last
Saturday night."
"Oh!" Paula's enthusiasm collapsed like a pricked
balloon. "I thought it would run forever. Isn't thatNO LOVELIER SPRING
[15]
just my luck! I've been crazy to see it. My sisters
simply raved!"
Rosalie lifted white lids fringed with golden
lashes.
"I loved it," she said. "I saw it during Christmas
week. I've seen every play your mother's done. I
think she's divine!"
Susie Lunt wouldn't say anything pleasant, of
course, but there was an admiring expression in her
eyes. Sally knew that was a tribute to Mother and
had nothing to do with her. Well, she didn't want
Susie Lunt to admire her. She could certainly do
without that!
"We'll have to see something else,” Paula said,
after a moment. "A musical comedy, maybe. You'll
come for luncheon, anyway, won't you?" The disks
of her glasses glittered up at Sally.
"I'm not sure that I can," Sally replied evasively.
"I'll probably be terribly busy." She hoped that the
words implied a spring vacation crowded with thrill-
ing engagements. "I'm practically certain," she elab-
orated, "not to have a free minute."
Paula accepted the evasion serenely. She seemed,
in fact, to be losing interest in the matter.
"Well, telephone me if you can," she said. "We're
in the directory. Jacob Hartman. Don't get Daddy's
offices by mistake. I'll be expecting a call."
She'd be disappointed, Sally thought, walking on
to her chair. She knew why Paula had invited her.
That "You'll come anyway, won't you?" had given[16] NO LOVELIER SPRING
her away. Paula had thought she would take the
party back-stage to meet Mother. Paula didn't really
want her. Well, she didn't want to go. She could
imagine what Paula's luncheon would be like, rich
and heavy and over-elaborate like Paula herself.
She didn't intend to waste a minute of vacation with
people like that, this spring vacation which, for some
reason she couldn't entirely explain, held an en-
chanting promise.
What was the promise it held? Sally settled her-
self in the deep plush chair and opened the book of
poetry in its gray and violet jacket. She didn't want
to read, though. The book lying open upon her lap
was merely a part of the picture, like her red leather
purse and the spray of gardenias wilting upon the
sill of the window. She felt a sense of betrayal in that
admission. Her fingers smoothed the white page in
apology, as though she was saying farewell to a
companion of her childhood.
No, not that. She was not bidding the book fare-
well. She was giving herself a chance to grow up to
it. The music of beautiful words no longer satisfied
her. She was determined to experience for herself
the emotions described in the verses which, six
months ago, she had read with wonder and delight.
She had to know their true meaning, the veiled
ecstasies and pain, the dark passions she could only
imagine.
It was going to happen! Her heart beat lightly
and quickly at the certainty she felt. The princeNO LOVELIER SPRING
[17]
was coming to release her, she thought, humiliated
that her lack of experience compelled her to express
her emotions in fairy-tale phrases. This spring vaca-
tion. In May she would be eighteen.
She'd had this feeling of expectancy since Christ-
mas, since Thanksgiving vacation, really, when she'd
spent three days with Maida at her home. It was
then she had been convinced that her faith in her-
self was justified, that she was beginning to be a
person.
She might have known before that. The fact that
Maida had selected her for an intimate friend was
proof enough, Maida Deering, a year older than
Sally, the most interesting girl in school with her
beauty and her cleverness and her romantic past.
It wasn't because she was Mother's daughter. Maida
hadn't even known her name that afternoon early
last fall, during a hockey game, when she'd come up
to Sally and said, "I choose you. Let's skip this
healthy exercise and have a little intelligent con-
versation."
She and Maida had been intimate friends ever
since, though Maida was a day-pupil because her
family thought they had to keep her at home after
she'd been expelled from the school in Virginia for
going down the fire-escape at night to meet a man
who was crazy about her. Not that it mattered.
They were together between classes and in the after-
noons. She had spent several week-ends with Maida.
The visit at Thanksgiving had been the most impor-[18]
NO LOVELIER SPRING
tant. She remembered every detail of the night they
had talked until dawn
...
They had had "dates" that evening, almost the
first Sally had ever had, except with boys in
Williamsburg when she visited Grandmother Shelby,
not the college boys there, but the younger and,
Sally suspected, unwilling sons of Grandmother's
friends. The "dates" were Yale men home in
Washington for vacation. She had felt awfully shy
dancing with them in the Deerings' game-room, try-
ing to think of something to talk about, wondering
if they thought her as uninteresting as she felt. She
had admired Maida's casual manner with them and
had despaired of ever attaining that breezy social
ease. The evening had been rather dreadful. She'd
been more alarmed than pleased when Maida had
said as they were getting ready for bed:
"Rod and Stan want to take us to the tea-dance
at the Shoreham to-morrow. How about it? Want
to go?"
"They don't want me," she'd said.
"Why not?" Maida had asked.
It had come out then in a burst of humiliation
she was unable to control.
"I'm so dark and scrawny," she'd wailed, feeling
that the world had come to an end. "I don't know
what to say to them."
"You make me sick!" Maida's voice had been
bracingly indignant. "Come here." She had plumped
her down on the seat before the dressing table andNO LOVELIER SPRING [19]
turned on all the lights. "Look at yourself," she
had commanded.
Sally hadn't seen anything very reassuring in the
mirror, just herself, more unattractive than usual
because of strain and tears.
"I'm looking," she'd said very meekly.
"You have beautiful eyes," Maida had pointed
out, "and the kind of a face that's called 'piquant'."
"Nobody ever called my face that," she'd said,
taking comfort, though, in the word.
"They would if you'd do your hair differently.
Look!" Maida had smoothed her hair from her
forehead and back behind her ears. It had changed
her, somehow. A ray of hope had brightened the
gloom.
"But I'm scrawny!" she'd repeated, seeing very
plainly in the mirror the contrast between Maida's
figure and her own.
"You aren't," Maida had insisted almost angrily.
"You aren't as thin as you were two months ago.
You're filling out, my child."
"That's milk and eggs from the school farm,"
she'd said, able, then, to smile. "The catalogue is
living up to its promise."
"All you need is self-confidence," Maida had con-
tinued. "You're more fun to be with than any girl
I've ever known. You think the right things are
funny or sad or lovely or ridiculous."
"Do I?" she'd asked a little breathlessly, seeing
her eyes brighten in the mirror.[20]
NO LOVELIER SPRING
"Do you?" Maida had repeated scoffingly. "Well,
if you won't take my word for it, Stan and Rod
both think you're attractive. Stan said you're a keen
dancer and Rod is crazy about your eyes."
"They couldn't," she'd protested, feeling her
cheeks grow warm. "I couldn't say anything to
them. I"
"Chattering is no proof of charm," Maida had
said. She'd looked at Sally appraisingly. "You have
a touch of dark magic," she'd concluded, changing
all of life for Sally. "You've been some time get-
ting around to it, but I've an idea you're going
to work considerable havoc in this ordinary
world"
•
"Brush you, Miss?" The porter's voice recalled
Sally to the Pullman. She rose obediently, unable to
shake off, immediately, the memory of Maida's
observations. "Six mo' minutes," the porter con-
tinued. "We're on time to the second."
The cheerful words caught and held her atten-
tion. Sally felt her heart leap and sink again with a
dreadful sickening thud. She was aware, then, of
movements about her, the preliminary bustle which
accompanied the slackening speed of the train. The
porter assembled her luggage. She watched him
helplessly, wanting to tell him to leave the familiar
hat box and dressing cases exactly where they were.
She was nearly there. In six more minutes she would
be in New York.
Five minutes, four minutes, three
•
..
Sally satNO LOVELIER SPRING
[21]
tense and still on the edge of the chair. She couldn't
do it. She wasn't ready. How had she been able to
discard so confidently the old Sally's disguises? She
wondered, fleetingly, if a butterfly felt as she did
now when it broke through its brown cocoon and
spread its wings in the sunlight. She tried to encour-
age herself with Maida's prediction, with all the
compliments she had ever received, with the delight
in her appearance that she'd felt in the dressing-
room.
Nothing helped very much. Sally mingled with the
stream of passengers moving toward the door, con-
scious in a dream-like way of Susie Lunt's giggle
behind her, of the scent of the fading gardenias, of
the lurching stop of the train. She had to go on.
No matter what happened to her she had to see it
through. Her heart beat heavily but there was,
mixed with fear and uncertainty, a sense of excite-
ment. She'd felt like this the first time she dove, on
Maida's dare, from the highest spring-board in the
pool at school. This, too, was a dare from Maida.
She had to go on.
The porter extended his hand. Sally stepped from
the box at the foot of the steps down onto the plat-
form. She drew a long valiant breath. Spring vaca-
tion had begun.CHAPTER II
Taxi
Paxi, Miss?" asked the red-cap carrying Sally's
bags.
"No," she replied. "You can leave me here.
There'll be someone to meet me."
He placed the luggage at her feet, accepted his
tip and hurried off with a gangling loose-jointed gait.
Sally stood near the train exit, her eyes, not so bright
and excited now, searching through the crowds eddy-
ing about her for a familiar figure. There was usu-
ally someone to meet her when she arrived in New
York; Mother, if she hadn't a matinee, or Maggie
McKim or one of Mother's friends. Whoever came
was almost certain to be late, though. Mother had
a way of not remembering train schedules accu-
rately. Perhaps she should have taken a taxi.
Waiting was a strain. If Mother had been at the
gate, if she could have made her first appearance
in a flurry of greetings, questions, arrangements
about her luggage, she might have carried off the
situation with some degree of poise. But that diffi-
cult first appearance was still an ordeal to be faced.
22NO LOVELIER SPRING [23]
She was afraid to go on alone to Mother's apart-
ment. There would be people there, almost certainly,
at this hour in the late afternoon. She knew what
would happen if she should walk into the living-
room unexpectedly, without having been met.
Mother would be terribly contrite about having
forgotten the time that her train arrived and
awfully glad to see her. She would ask her a dozen
questions all at once and call Maggie to get her
something to eat and Annette to take her bags. Then
she would hold her off for inspection and ask in her
lovely laughing voice, her nose wrinkling in the way
Sally knew so well, "What have you done to your-
self? You adorable baby! Sally, you're growing up!"
The people who were always in Mother's apart-
ment, surrounding her like a musical comedy chorus,
would make bright remarks and turn Sally's effort
to be herself into an amusing "situation." Sally's
spirits quailed at the thought. Oh no, she couldn't
do that!
Time passed very slowly. Sally glanced at her
watch. She felt small and unimportant standing in
the vast vaulted concourse of the station. Lonely,
too, and unwanted. Susie Lunt's mother was meet-
ing Susie. Sally was sure the woman in the dark
tailored suit was Susie's mother. She had, though
she was very smartly groomed, the same weasel look
that Susie had. It was strange to think that anyone
in the world cared enough about Susie to meet her[24]
NO LOVELIER SPRING
at the station. Susie Lunt was a mean, deceitful,
little- Oh, well-
Two boys were meeting Rosalie Archer. Sally
felt a slight twinge of envy watching Rosalie's
escorts struggle for the possession of her bags. But
only a very slight twinge. In a moment she was able
to despise their clumsy gallantry, to feel superior to
Rosalie, so flutteringly delighted with it all.
Boys! Sally loathed them. A series of pictures
passed through her mind, familiar, often reviewed.
In one a man and a girl sat in low chairs before an
open fire, their faces turned toward each other. In
another a man and a girl leaned against the rail of a
liner, waving farewell to friends on shore. In a
third a girl and a man floated in a gondola beneath
an Italian moon, the scarlet tulle of her "robe de
style" spraying over his evening clothes. The girl in
each was small and dark and beautiful, a glorified
Sally. The man's features varied, but he was con-
sistently "older" and "interesting" with a romantic
occupation and a scar over his eye. Boys! Pooh!
Sally made a mental grimace.
Paula Hartman was being welcomed by an im-
pressive delegation. The man with the red com-
plexion was her father, of course, and the stout
woman in light beige silk under summer ermine was
her mother. The two younger women were "Bea"
and "Gwen," Paula's sisters. There were half a
dozen children, too, a pop-eyed dog, a chauffeur in a
plum-colored uniform collecting Paula's luggage.NO LOVELIER SPRING [25]
The twinge Sally felt as she saw Paula drawn with
smiles and cries and embraces into the demonstra-
tive family group was harder, this time, to dismiss.
They were ridiculous people, like drawings in the
New Yorker. Maida would have been scornfully
amusing about them. They looked happy, though,
and good-natured and fond of each other. It would
be fun -
Sally checked the thought resolutely. She didn't
mind, any longer, not being part of a family. She
knew something about families, now, since she and
Maida had been intimate friends. Families were
dreadful. They tried to prevent your leading your
own life and thought you ungrateful if you had an
idea or an ambition of which they didn't approve.
Maida was going to break away from her family
when she was twenty and came into possession of
the money her grandfather had left her. She was
going to travel all over the world and have a won-
derful life, though she supposed her family would
trail her with letters and cablegrams and private
detectives.
Maida envied her, Sally, because she had only
Mother and Grandmother Shelby, of course, and
her father's relatives who didn't count because she
almost never saw any of them. Maida said it was
much less difficult to escape from one parent than
from an entire family of brothers and sisters and
uncles and aunts and cousins all fighting each other
most of the time but united in trying to ruin her[26]
NO LOVELIER SPRING
life. To escape was important, Maida said. She told
Sally, in discussions filled with confusing psycho-
logical references, that she would never be a real
person until she escaped from Mother.
But she didn't want to escape. Sally felt a familiar
heaviness in her breast. Maida didn't understand
how she felt about Mother. She'd never told Maida.
She'd never told anyone. Her love for Mother was
something she couldn't put into words. It was there,
inside of her, warm and glowing within the shell of
reticence and pretense she had grown to protect it.
She didn't want Mother to know how much she
loved her. Mother was tender-hearted and generous
and never, if she could help it, hurt anyone's feel-
ings. She'd be awfully unhappy not to be able to
return Sally's love with an equal measure of love
and affection. And she couldn't, of course. That
wasn't to be expected.
Perhaps Maida was right. Sally shifted her
weight from one foot to the other. Perhaps she
could never become a person because she loved
Mother too much. Even her delight in the way she
was getting to look had a great deal to do with
Mother. She wanted Mother to be proud of her.
She wanted to compensate Mother for the burden
she'd been. She had to make amends, partially at
least, for having been born.
Thinking of Mother brought a memory, never
far distant, into the foreground of her mind. She'd
been four years old the first time she'd heardNO LOVELIER SPRING
[27]
Mother tell Grandmother Shelby, in speaking of her
father's death, "If the baby hadn't come so soon!
Ned worked so desperately hard. He hadn't strength
enough to fight pneumonia ..."
If the baby hadn't come! It was her fault that
her father had died when she was ten months old,
leaving Mother, who loved him, alone in the world
with a baby to support. It wasn't to be supposed
that even a brilliant and beautiful daughter, the sort
of person she meant to become, could entirely com-
pensate Mother for that
That wasn't true, of course. It was a childish
idea which she should long ago have outgrown. She
knew that it wasn't true. Nothing that had hap-
pened before her birth could have been her fault.
She'd believed it so firmly in childhood, though.
That was why it kept returning, not often now,
only at times like this when she felt lonely and
unwanted. She wouldn't think of it again. She would
outgrow being so sensitive and silly and young. She
was going to have a wonderful vacation
The brief flare of reviving enthusiasm dimmed
and faded. You had to consider facts. This holiday
which, in anticipation had held an enchanting
promise, wasn't beginning very well. Dreams and
reality were quite different. You couldn't close your
mind entirely to that sobering truth.
No use waiting here any longer. This time
Mother had forgotten entirely. She'd have to take
a taxi. Sally glanced about for a red-cap. It was[28] NO LOVELIER SPRING
then that she noticed, consciously, the man in the
good-looking country tweeds standing beside her.
She had been absently aware of him for the past
few moments. Subconsciously, through the thoughts
absorbing her attention, she had watched him walk-
ing leisurely back and forth in a restricted area near
the train gate. It was his unhurried manner which
had first impressed her, she thought. He seemed to
be the only person in the station, beside herself,
who wasn't scurrying frantically in some definite
direction. She noticed, now, another mark of dis-
tinction. In comparison with the pallid faces all
about her his deep even tan was startlingly con-
spicuous. Sally liked it. He looked as though he
lived always in the sun.
He was looking away from her, standing quite
still, whistling cheerfully under his breath. Sally
examined his features in a series of swift darting
glances. He wasn't young, she decided. He might
be even older than thirty-five. There was a fine sun-
burst of lines raying out from the eye she saw in
profile. His face was lean and he had a wide
pleasant-looking mouth. His hair, under the tilted
brim of a tan felt hat, was a lightish brown and
glossy like straw. Sunburned, she thought, not
merely blond. He wasn't conventionally handsome
but he was good looking in a nice country sort of
way. Sally found herself glancing up to see if he
could possibly have a scar over his eyes.
At that moment he glanced down. She had justNo LOVELIER SPRING
[29]
time to notice that his eyes were brown with yellow
flecks before, in confusion, she looked down at her
red leather purse. Breathing was difficult for a
moment. She'd thought, she'd been almost sure that
he was going to speak to her. There had been an
inquiring expression in his eyes. Her first impulse
was to snatch up her bags and run.
She forced herself to stand perfectly still. She'd
always hoped that this would happen to her. She'd
dreamed of meeting an interesting "older" man in
some unconventional way. How could she expect to
have romantic adventures if the first attractive man
who looked at her threw her into a panic? Maida
would know exactly what to do next. Maida had
romantic adventures
He was looking at her. Sally felt in every part of
her body the inquiring intensity of his glance. She
hoped, with one part of her mind, that he noticed
her Creole coloring while with another, she tried

much fonder of her handsome, happy-go-lucky

office.

Phe ferry moved stolidly across the harbor
toward the city. Martin and Sally stood lean-
ing against the forward rail, watching the small
waves break and curl back in frills of foam from
the snub-nosed bow of the boat. There were few
passengers besides themselves and they were assem-
bled in more sheltered quarters for, though the day
had been sunny, as evening approached the wind
was edged with a chill. An Italian with an accordion,
scenting a tip, hovered near them, playing with
more energy than skill a succession of wheezing
tunes.
Martin, at intervals, glanced down at Sally,
standing beside him, the collar of her top-coat but-
toned up under her chin, a fly-away scrap of a hat
perched high on her wind-blown hair. She had, in
the past few moments, retreated into some hiding
place within herself reserved for shyness or for
thought. What had made her shy? What was she
thinking? He could not see her face, only the top
of her head and her hands in chamois gloves clasped
quietly on the rail.

whispered. "I can't tell anyone-not even you."
At that moment the ferry warped into the dock.
The resulting jolt caught her off balance. His arm
went around her to steady her and he felt that she
was trembling.
"You're shivering," he said self-reproachfully,
aware that the trembling increased in the moment
that she rested against him. "I've let you get cold."
She neither accepted nor rejected the explanation.
He led her off the ferry, along the dock, through
the waiting-room and out into the street. She walked
beside him silently, looking straight ahead, seem-
ing scarcely to breathe. Had she? Was it pos-
sible? He forced disturbing conjectures from his
mind. She hadn't. It wasn't possible. She was a little
girl, Laurel's daughter, a dear and appealing child.
"I'm sorry I can't take you home," he said, sig-
naling a cab. "I'm having dinner at the Tennants'.
Mr. Tennant is entertaining representatives from
an English fruit concern. Do you mind awfully,
Sally?"
"No, I don't mind." She would not look at him.
She poked at a bit of broken cement in the pave-
ment with the toe of her slipper. The cab slithered
in to the curb.
"Don't let this fellow kidnap you," he said, curi-
ously reluctant to send her off alone. "Have a good
dinner and go to bed early. I'll call you to-morrow."
She looked out at him through the lowered win-[138]
NO LOVELIER SPRING
dow of the cab. He saw to his complete amazement
that the light, so abruptly dimmed, was shining
again.
"Sell tons of oranges to the Englishmen," she
said with a gay little gesture of farewell. "And
Martin!" she cried as the cab jolted into motion.
"Tell Richard Tennant that I know what a jib-
boom is!"
Martin watched the cab until it was out of sight,
the slightly bewildered expression her parting mes-

a self-mocking smile. He was a fatuous ass, he told
himself, striking at nothing in particular with his
stick. All his conjectures were mercifully false.
Young Tennant! Sally properly considered him an
old man. He hailed a taxi and sank back against the
frayed musty-smelling upholstery. Mingled with the
relief he felt was a recognizable sense of discom-
fiture. If he was to be Sally's step-father, he'd have
to learn to make better guesses than that.CHAPTER X

ally sat, cross-legged, in a chair drawn close
to the window, a pad on her knees, the point
of her fountain pen moving nimbly across the paper
forming the angular back-hand letters she had culti-

the view from the long front windows now very fa-
miliar. The storm Martin predicted yesterday had
begun sometime during the night. Fog horns sounded
at intervals, bleating dismally like animals left out
and lost in the fog. It was a cold and depressing
day.
Sally did not feel depressed. The crimson drapes
at the windows were cheerful. A small fire burned
in the grate beneath the carved white mantel. Re-

and the andirons. The white rug before the hearth
looked faintly pink in the rosy glow of the fire.
When her glance fell upon it, Sally was reminded of

vacation, actually boasting and gloating for all she
was worth.
She referred from time to time to the letter she

tilted hazel eyes. "Mother spends all her time fuss-

Poor Maida! Sally sighed loyally but a smile

was considerate enough not to speak of it directly.
Now it was Sally's turn. Maida who had half a
dozen men in love with her and more invitations
than she could possibly accept, was having a foul
time in Atlantic City while she, Sally, was treading
the primrose path with complete abandon.
It was unkind to gloat. But she couldn't help it.
Anyway Maida would be delighted. People were

Phe orchestra played the overture to the third
act of "Tristan," the curtain parted in velvet
ripples, and the shepherd boy, scanning a sea with-
out a sail, piped his plaintively hopeless song.
Martin settled down into the plush chair beside
Sally's and gave his rambling attention to the stage.
This portion of the opera was familiar. He had a
record of the shepherd's song, one of the heteroge-
neous assortment he had collected during the years.
How had he come by it? From some faint clue the
music gave him, he reconstructed a memory of the
first time he had heard "Tristan," just before he'd
gone to France. Good Lord! Eighteen years ago!
His father and mother had come to New York
to see him off and had brought Caroline Temple.
He supposed he and Caroline had begrudged an
evening at the opera when they might have been
dancing at the Biltmore, he in his first tailor-made
uniform and she, a dark little thing, more conven-
tionally pretty than Sally, in the sort of evening
dresses she wore then, all ruffles and rose-buds and
171[172] NO LOVELIER SPRING
innocent allurement. They had gone to please his
mother, perhaps. She loved music and was terribly
cut up because he was going to France. He re-
membered sitting beside Caroline, holding her hand
under cover of the darkness, completely carried away
by the most passionate love-music in the world.
He'd seen Caroline in the chesty Isolde with her
blonde braids and jeweled girdle and himself in the
manly Tristan bidding his love farewell. He'd
thought Caroline must be feeling as he felt and
bending toward her as the curtain closed, he had
asked a question.
He remembered her shining dark eyes, her parted
lips, her look of suspended breathing. He re-
membered, too, the reply she'd made when he asked
her what she was thinking.
"We can be married as soon as you come home,
can't we?" she'd asked in the little girl voice he'd
adored. "While the boys are still in uniform, I mean.
A military wedding looks nicer in pictures than the
usual kind."
Well, that was Caroline. He'd been surprised
when she'd sent the record to France, packed in a
box between badly knitted mufflers and lumpy socks
made for a dwarf. One of the instincts he'd often
mistaken for intelligence had prompted her to send
it, perhaps. He was still in love with her and he
had been greatly touched but before he'd had time
to get a portable Victrola to play it on he'd had
her letter telling him that she was going to marryNO LOVELIER SPRING [173]
Phillip Ashley from Charleston who could provide
a military wedding immediately since he'd never
gotten farther than tending mules in a cavalry train-
ing camp in Georgia.
The music of "Tristan" had not lost its old
enchantment. It was magnificent, exciting, but, to
him, less emotionally absorbing than that night
eighteen years ago when Caroline Temple had sat
beside him. He heard it today, not unmoved, but
with a detachment which made thought possible.
What was Sally making of it? He turned to look
at her in the dim light of the orchestra circle.
She was looking straight ahead and, as he saw
it in profile, her expression was exalted. If he asked
her what she was thinking, what reply would she
make? Something as trivial as Caroline's had been
that night eighteen years ago, perhaps. No, that
was unfair to Sally. She was sensitive. There were
depths in her nature which Caroline never had. Her
instincts were informed by intelligence, groping, per-
haps, uncertain, but fundamentally sound.
His eyes, but not his attention, returned to the
stage. He had been a little surprised when Sally had
suggested that he come with her to this benefit
matinee of "Tristan and Isolde" for which Laurel,
with her accustomed generosity, had bought half a
dozen tickets. He'd have thought there were gayer
things she might have wanted to do this last after-
noon of the ambiguous, but pleasant, companion-[174]
NO LOVELIER SPRING
ship he'd shared with her, though the weather was
still cold and wet.
She didn't know this was their last afternoon of

His thoughts turned to the business negotiations

[175]
ability of underselling if he weren't about to assume

Money enough, however. Money enough, in spite

Next fall-Laurel would be with him then at

he lose her eventually? That was a chance he would
have to take.
He'd never before, in his relations with the
women he might have married, questioned his ability
to provide happiness of a sort. He had selfishly con-
sidered only his side of the situation. Even with
Mary Hamilton. He'd been at the point of asking
Mary to marry him before his visit to New York
last winter. What if he had? He liked her and
admired the way she'd carried on the business of
the groves bordering his after her husband's death.
He hadn't loved Mary romantically. He'd thought[176] NO LOVELIER SPRING
that romantic love was a commodity to be sold in
novels and verses and music like this. He hadn't,
for ten years at least, thought of it as something
which might actually exist.
One evening with Laurel had altered that con-
viction, the night they had driven until dawn in
a taxi through Central Park. His former self-
sufficient, easy-going existence had been shattered as
certainly as though an earthquake had destroyed
his home and uprooted his groves. He had to have
Laurel, if the rest of his life was to have any mean-

remaining years of his life were hers. She held in
her slender vivacious hands his happiness or his
ultimate defeat

do, content because the other is there?"
It was not a brilliant analogy, he thought. But it

Martin didn't love her. He'd been very gentle

If they hadn't let her stumble blindly into this situ-

fall in love with Martin-
Yes, she had. Honesty compelled her to admit

Strange that it should have been he instead of

Prince. He was young and handsome as princes in

ferently-It was all very confusing.
1
What had happened couldn't be helped. She must

She knew how to count and make change. She could[248] NO LOVELIER SPRING
be a waitress. She stopped to look in through a

The raw chops in the window made her feel ill.

She'd hate to sit in a window, though, and have

and asked that dark little man if he needed help.
She felt covered with goose-flesh at the thought.
She oughtn't to feel like this. She and Maida had
often talked about getting jobs, living alone, being

[249]
never considered how they'd feel if they'd had to

Four young people dashed out of a shop, two

"Excuse me," he said, giving her a quick merry

Sally watched them wistfully as they linked arms

She didn't want to be free and independent, not
completely, not in the way that Maida meant. She
had no particular talents or ambitions. The idea of

granted. She'd had gaiety and excitement and men
falling in love with her since she was fourteen.
The four young people whom Sally watched dis-

No, she wasn't like Maida. She thought of that

Daurel sat before the desk in the small room
off Maggie's which served as an office. The
room was dark in the late afternoon when the sun-
light had moved beyond the one northerly window.
A lamp in a business-like metal shade was lit at each
end of the flat, strictly utilitarian, varnished pine
desk.
The additional furnishings of the room were as
severely plain as the desk, a metal filing cabinet, a
small safe reminiscent of old-fashioned village
stores, the swivel chair in which Laurel sat, an
armchair beside the window upholstered in faded
green rep, straight hanging curtains of turkey-red
calico, a dark green rug on the floor. Laurel had
often thought that the room was like the pilot room
of a luxurious liner, for it was here, in an atmos-
phere far different from the rest of the apartment,
that Maggie planned and checked and supervised to
keep her life running smoothly and true to a charted
course.
Something of that was in her mind as she went
286NO LOVELIER SPRING
[287]
through the drawers of the desk, sorting, destroy-
ing, stacking the papers she wanted to keep in an
uneven pile. The contents of the drawers were neatly
arranged with labels and clips and elastic bands.
The contracts, the clippings, the canceled checks,
might have been recipes for jam and gingerbread
instead of the miscellany connected with the busi-
ness of being a successful actress. The drawers had
looked much the same when the desk had stood in
the rooming-house where she and Ned had lived
during the scant two years of their life together,
only then the papers in the elastic bands had been
receipts for room-rent paid, tax collectors' vouchers,
communications from the butcher, the baker, the-
no, not the candle-stick maker, the gas and electric
company of downtown Manhattan.
The desk would have to go. If they'd been pack-
ing to move to Florida, they might have sent it
there. A quiver of pain passed over Laurel's face,
white and drained of animation, above the white
collar on the simple frock of yellow linen. They
couldn't take it to Hollywood and she was unable
to think beyond that anticipated move. The desk
was precious to Maggie and, through Maggie, to
her. Better put it in storage. Maggie, under her
prickly ways and shrewd common-sense, was as sen-
timental as a Valentine. It would probably hurt her
to see the desk carried off in a junk dealer's truck.
The work of clearing the drawers did not pro-
gress rapidly. Laurel found things she hadn't seen[288]
NO LOVELIER SPRING
for years, small objects which stirred memories, not

She looked at it closely, moved to see Ned's dark

a dream.

But she didn't want to go. Fear squeezed her
